How might an internship evolve into a career in data science?
2026-09-03

How might an internship evolve into a career in data science?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Accenture’s Andrew Kelly explores the early days of his career amid Ireland’s data science landscape and how others might forge a similar career path. Andrew Kelly, a senior manager in AI and data at Accenture, was undertaking a degree in engineering when he first started out as an intern at the organisation. He explained that it was the first time that he had real exposure to data science, through working with data to find patterns and produce insights. He told SiliconRepublic.com, “I had always enjoyed maths and quantitative subjects, but this was different. It was hands-on, applied and had a clear commercial reason behind it. Something clicked. I went back to college, completed my master’s in engineering, but already knew where I wanted to end up.” Soon after, Kelly re-joined Accenture as a graduate and immediately began to work once again within the realm of analytics, but also started to branch out into business intelligence reporting, data engineering, GenAI, financial services and, increasingly, banking. Kelly said, “In recent years my focus has shifted from being a data practitioner to helping banking clients build the foundations for AI at scale – the platforms, the governance, the risk frameworks and the responsible AI processes that have to sit behind all of it – while simultaneously trying to deliver real, measurable value quickly enough to prove it works.” As Kelly finds it, Ireland is “in a promising but unfinished position” within a space that has allowed for the sharp acceleration of AI adoption. According to a previous report published by Accenture, the number of Irish employees using GenAI tools daily is almost triple the figure noted just two years ago. However, he is of the opinion that this has yet to translate into a more visible and effective organisational transformation, noting it is really only taking effect at an individual level, rather than across whole companies. “What I see consistently in organisations is the treatment of AI as a technology initiative rather than a business reinvention. There is a strong temptation to automate isolated steps in a process, to take a multi-step workflow and automate part of it,” said Kelly. “That is not transformation. The organisations that will pull ahead are those asking a fundamentally different question: ‘How does AI change how we work end-to-end?’ or ‘How does it change how we interact with customers?’ That requires process reinvention, not point automation.” There is a gap shown in the research, he finds, with only around 10pc of Irish organisations having reached what Accenture would classify as ‘scaler’ status – that is, where AI is embedded in core operations. The majority are still experimenting and integrating, largely in silos. “The data, governance and legacy infrastructure challenges are real constraints. But the deeper issue is often strategic clarity. AI investment needs to be value-led, tied to top-level business outcomes, not driven by the technology itself. “The encouraging signal is that Ireland has the right ingredients – talent, infrastructure, regulatory framework and genuine national ambition. The question is execution at scale, and that clock is ticking.” Though it wasn’t all that long ago, much has changed in the data science and wider STEM space since Kelly first began his career. He explained that at the start of his journey, work was often centred on the development of predictive models in code and running data science projects that served specific parts of the business. Conversations were “largely technical” and outcomes were “meaningful but relatively contained in their organisational reach”. “What has changed fundamentally is the scope of impact. GenAI still requires deep expertise to build and govern well, but its reach extends far beyond those who build it. It is permeating processes across entire organisations, touching roles, workflows and customer interactions that traditional machine learning never reached in the same way.” Skill expectations have somewhat transformed too. Nowadays, workforce-wide AI readiness has emerged as a critical capability. “AI literacy can no longer sit only with the teams building the technology. It must be distributed across the business. The thing that has not changed is the pace of change itself. It has always been fast in technology, but it is accelerating in ways that organisations must actively manage,” said Kelly. “The technical foundation matters – understanding data engineering, machine learning, platform architecture and governance well enough to have credible conversations with both practitioners and senior clients. But in my day-to-day, the skills I lean on most are not technical ones.” Instead, he regularly utilises a broad skillset that enables him to navigate complex environments with professionals in regulation, risk, tech, business and senior leadership, “often simultaneously and with competing priorities”. He added, “The ability to translate between technical teams and business decision-makers, between what AI can do and what it should do, is probably the skill I use most.” But most of all, the attribute that often stands out the most in a career setting is not a perfect track record, but a display of genuine interest. Kelly himself came into his initial role as an intern, moving from engineering into data science, and he often wondered in the early days if his technical profile compared sufficiently with those of people who had studied computer science or statistics directly. “That was misplaced. What I had was curiosity and a willingness to learn and, looking back, that counted for far more. The second thing, and this is something I still have to remind myself of, is to make peace with not knowing everything. In AI especially, that feeling of being slightly behind the curve is essentially universal. “Everyone is learning with this technology. The organisations and individuals who are pulling ahead are not necessarily the ones with the most prior knowledge. They are the ones with the highest aptitude and appetite for learning continuously as things change. “What I look for when I hire is exactly that – the ability to pick things up, adapt and grow. Skills can be acquired. That orientation, that genuine curiosity and resilience is harder to teach. If you have it, the rest tends to follow.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Five Irish experts among 421 awarded €705m in ERC Starting Grants
2026-09-03

Five Irish experts among 421 awarded €705m in ERC Starting Grants

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
From left: Dr Casey Platnich and Dr Gabriela Kronemberger. Image: Trinity College Dublin Nearly 5,000 proposals were submitted this year, highlighting ‘Europe’s growing appeal for frontier research’, said the EU’s start-ups commissioner. €705m has been allocated to 421 early-career researchers of more than 50 different nationalities for research work in affiliated countries under the European Research Council’s (ERC) Starting Grants initiative. Five awards collectively worth multiple millions of euros were given to experts at Irish organisations for their research into innovative drug development, and health and safety. A record 4,807 grant proposals were submitted for 2026 – up 22pc since last year. Only 8.8pc of the applicants were successful in receiving funding. 169 US-based researchers applied, with 20 successful awardees now gearing up to move to Europe to continue their work. A Starting Grant provides up to €1.5m over a period of up to five years. As part of the ‘Choose Europe’ initiative that aims to attract and retain global scientific talent, the ERC can award up to €2m for applicants moving to Europe. “Their decision [to move] reflects Europe’s growing appeal for frontier research and is an encouraging sign that the EU’s Choose Europe initiative is helping to attract the next generation of scientific talent while reinforcing Europe’s position as a destination of choice for excellent research,” said Ekaterina Zaharieva, the European commissioner for start-ups, research and innovation. The selected projects will cover a broad spectrum of research fields from life sciences and engineering to social sciences and humanities, according to the ERC. Two researchers from Trinity College Dublin, Dr Casey Platnich and Dr Gabriela Kronemberger, have each received €1.5m for their five-year projects and to build their own research teams. Platnich, an assistant professor based in Trinity’s School of Chemistry, will lead a project called ‘Snaprr’, which aims to transform how scientists detect and understand RNA viruses. Current technologies often miss important details about viral chemistry that can tell us how pathogens spread, evolve and respond to medicines, the university said. Snappr aims to develop a new way to study viral RNA using tiny nanopore sensors roughly a thousand times smaller than the width of a human hair. Meanwhile, Dr Kronemberger, based in the university’s School of Engineering, will lead ‘Smart-Meniscus’, a project integrating 3D bioprinting, stem-cell engineering and gene delivery technologies to develop an approach that targets the structural, cellular and immunological challenges of meniscus repair. “Becoming an ERC awardee is the greatest honour of my career,” said Kronemberger. “I am deeply grateful to the ERC for this opportunity, which comes at a pivotal moment for me, and I am excited to begin this project, which will allow me to build my own research team and pursue ambitious new directions in tissue engineering to address a critical, unmet need.” From left: Dr Alan Seltzer and Dr Seungkuk Ahn. Image: Angela Halpin University College Dublin (UCD) researchers also won two grants collectively worth nearly €4m. Dr Alan Seltzer from the UCD School of Earth Sciences has received nearly €2.5m to study naturally occurring gases dissolved in groundwater behaving like “chemical fingerprints” in order to hopefully understand how groundwater systems have responded to environmental change over much longer timescales than direct observations would otherwise allow. €1.5m has been allocated to Dr Seungkuk Ahn from UCD’s Charles Institute of Dermatology for a project called ‘Design-ECM’, which will investigate species-driven dimorphism – or sex-specific characteristics – in biomaterials and cell biology across materials science, bioengineering and nanobiotechnology. Success in this project could reshape the design of future preclinical models to “seriously reckon with species and sex of biomaterials and cells”, UCD said. Meanwhile, Dr Alan Costello from the National Institute for Bioprocessing Research and Training has won a fifth grant award for Ireland. “The creativity and ambition of this new generation of grantees are inspiring. Over 50 nationalities are represented amongst these researchers who will carry out their work across Europe,” ERC president Prof Maria Leptin said. “ERC Starting Grants will give them independence and support early on in their careers, needed for them to push scientific boundaries. This is the best investment we can make to further strengthen our research base and Europe’s future.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Apple facing £2bn UK legal challenge over ATT policy fairness
2026-09-03

Apple facing £2bn UK legal challenge over ATT policy fairness

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
The legal claim is led by ATT Collective Action Limited, whose director, Ann Pope, is a former senior director for antitrust at the UK’s Competition and Markets Authority. Apple is to face a £2bn collective legal action at the UK’s Competition Appeal Tribunal (CAT) brought on behalf of thousands of app developers over the fairness of its App Tracking Transparency (ATT) framework. Apple’s ATT policy requires third-party apps to obtain user permission twice before they can track iPhone and iPad users across company apps and websites, but does not apply the same requirement to Apple’s own first-party apps. The legal claim argues that the “double consent requirement for third-party apps is harmful to developers, whose business model relies on the sale of advertising space, as well as to advertisers and advertising intermediation platforms”. The claim is led by ATT Collective Action Limited, whose director, Ann Pope, is a former senior director for antitrust at the UK’s Competition and Markets Authority. The claim’s organisers said: “The implementation of ATT on 26 April 2021 fundamentally altered how many developers provide their services, but these changes were not implemented fairly.” They argue that “Apple abused its power and discriminated against app developers by implementing ATT unfairly and without proper consultation, imposing stricter requirements on third-party app developers than on its own services and leaving UK app developers unable to adapt their business models in time to avoid significant losses”. The US tech giant, according to the claim, applies “less burdensome requirements to its own advertising and data collection activities than those imposed on third-party app developers”, giving Apple’s advertising ecosystem a competitive advantage while imposing additional restrictions on businesses that depend on its App Store. “While Apple introduced ATT as a privacy measure designed to give users greater control over their data, it was one rule for third parties that depend on Apple’s ecosystem and another rule for Apple”, according to the claimants, who allege that the policy “caused significant losses across the UK app development sector because of the reduced value of advertising and the increased cost of attracting new users”. “Privacy is an important protection for consumers, but it should be applied fairly and in a way that ensures businesses of all sizes can compete on a level playing field,” said Pope, an economist and competition law specialist. “It cannot become a reason for digital platforms to play by one set of rules while forcing app developers to play by another. “The ATT policy was implemented without the transparency and objectivity you would expect from a company in Apple’s position, and it resulted in very significant harm to businesses that depend on Apple as a gatekeeper.” In a statement provided to SiliconRepublic.com, Apple described the lawsuit’s allegation that ATT gives the company a competitive advantage as false, and stated that a number of authorities have previously voiced support for the ATT framework – including the CMA and the European Commission. “At Apple, we believe privacy is a fundamental human right, and we created App Tracking Transparency to give users a simple way to control whether apps have permission to track their activity across other companies’ apps and websites,” a spokesperson for the company told SiliconRepublic.com. “Apple is bound by the exact same requirements as all developers under ATT, and this feature has been embraced by our customers and praised by privacy advocates and data protection authorities around the world, including in the UK. We disagree with these claims, and will continue to defend strong privacy protections for our users.” Last month, Germany’s competition authority ruled that Apple must change ATT consent prompts over concerns that the system deliberately produces different consent experiences for non-Apple competing apps, services and advertising activities. Under the ruling, Apple must redesign its consent pop-ups for third-party apps. This includes the removal of discouraging language and symbols, as well as ensuring that pop-ups are visually and linguistically ​neutral. The recommended changes, which Apple agreed to implement, are expected to apply across most other EU countries. In December, Italy’s competition authority fined Apple more than €98.6m after finding that the company abused its “super-dominant position” in the app distribution market through ATT, while France’s competition watchdog fined the US tech giant €150m over ATT in April 2025. Last month, Apple’s Big Tech rival and parent company of Google Alphabet agreed to pay £260m in order to bring an end to a class action lawsuit at the CAT over whether or not it has abused its market position to impose unfair fees on developers creating apps for use with its Android OS. MTU’s Rubicon Centre marks 20 years of supporting entrepreneurs

From left: Josette O'Mullane, Jim Woulfe, Maggie Cusack and Paul Healy. Over two decades of operations, Irish innovation hub the Rubicon Centre has enabled and supported more than 500 start-ups, with its clients raising more than €160m in public and private equity, according to the centre. Founded in 2006 and based at Munster Technological University (MTU) Cork, the hub aims to encourage and support entrepreneurs, high-potential start-ups and scaling businesses via incubation programmes, mentorship, investment and industry connections. A primary goal of the centre is to foster job creation and economic growth. The centre’s 20th anniversary was marked at an event yesterday (2 September) at the MTU Arena attended by Rubicon alumni, entrepreneurs and enterprise stakeholders for reflection on its history and future role in supporting businesses. Rubicon’s alumni include the founders of Cork-based vehicle development company Hibra design, which was developed through Enterprise Ireland’s New Frontiers programme at the centre. In February of this year, Hibra earned a place at the western Europe regional finals of the Global Start-up Awards, having been shortlisted in the Green-tech Start-up of the Year category. Also among Rubicon’s alumni are Swyft Energy’s Adrian Casey and Joy Freyne. Swyft Energy is a solar photo-voltaic and boiler installation company that was acquired by Bord Gáis Energy in 2024. Commenting on the 20-year milestone, Paul Healy, the manager of the Rubicon Centre at MTU, said, “Over the past 20 years, the Rubicon has become an important part of the entrepreneurship and business ecosystem in Cork and the wider region, supporting people from the earliest stages of developing an idea through to established businesses looking to grow and scale. “Every year, people come through our doors with an idea and a decision to take that next step. In many ways, they are ‘crossing the Rubicon’. Our role is to help them navigate what comes next through training, mentoring, and connections to investors, business networks, and the wider commercial and R&D expertise available across MTU. “As we look to the next 20 years, we see a real opportunity to build on that network and work with more established SMEs with the ambition to scale, grow and adapt.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. United Nations Concedes That Efforts to Arrest Climate Change Have Failed, and the Planet Is Now Spiraling Into Environmental Catastrophe

United Nations Concedes That Efforts to Arrest Climate Change Have Failed, and the Planet Is Now Spiraling Into Environmental Catastrophe

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. With so many climate projections and mitigation strategies eyeing far-away years like 2050, it can be tempting to assume there’s still time to reverse the worst impacts of an ever-warming planet. Unfortunately, that’s no longer in the cards — at least according to the United Nations Environment Programme, which just released a dismal report calling for the “world to face the inevitability of exceeding global warming of 1.5 degrees Celsius” above pre-industrial levels. That’s significant, because the 1.5 degree threshold has long been associated with a range of grim climate tipping points at which the planet will experience irreversible consequences such as the “breakdowns of major ocean circulation systems, abrupt thawing of boreal permafrost, and collapse of tropical coral reef systems,” per a UN climate explainer. The new UN paper, coming on the heels of the hottest summer on record and pessimistically titled “Limiting Overshoot,” identifies the consequences of this reality, and recommends some immediate actions to keep things from spiraling even further. Though most scientists agree we’re hovering around the 1.4 degree point at the moment, the UN notes that even in the best case scenario, where “all climate commitments and policies are implemented, warming is projected to reach 1.8 degrees Celsius at a minimum.” If we follow our current track, however, the report highlights the distinct possibility of hitting 2.6 degrees by the year 2100. That catastrophic future is far from certain, although drastic intervention is becoming increasingly urgent if we hope to stave it off. Underscoring the severity of the present moment, the UN frames the situation in terms of maintaining warming at the 1.5 degree mark — no longer a theoretical ceiling, but the new floor going forward. Doing so, the report authors write, depends “on achieving sustained net-negative CO2 emissions at global scale in the second half of this century, and potentially further reductions of non-CO2 emissions.” “Decisions taken this decade will determine whether viable options remain to gradually reverse warming back to 1.5 degrees Celsius,” they explain. Speaking to CBC News, UN Environment Programme executive director Inger Andersen said “let’s be very clear, 1.5 target is still the goal.” “But now we need to approach it differently from above,” Andersen explained. “Every tenth of a degree we avoid still means less damage.” More on climate change: It’s Official: This Is the Hottest America Has Ever Been

China Installing Huge Facility That Turns Plastic Waste Into Jet Fuel
2026-09-03

China Installing Huge Facility That Turns Plastic Waste Into Jet Fuel

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Around the world, the demand for cleaner alternatives to petroleum-based jet fuel is rising. By 2030, the European Union will require a minimum sustainable aviation fuel (SAF) blend of 6 percent, which will rise to 70 percent by 2050. Countries like the United Kingdom, Singapore, Japan, and Brazil are instituting similar policies, putting pressure on players in the global energy trade to ease reliance on crude oil. SAF can come from all kinds of recycled waste byproducts like plant material, animal fat, and used cooking oil — the latter of which has already become a major source for existing SAF supplies. Unfortunately, the flow of extra spent fry oil is already slowing to a trickle as refiners swoop in to secure the gloop. That’s where Chinese company Niutech comes in. In a new press release, first spotted by Interesting Engineering, Niutech announced a massive contract with an anonymous “global energy major” to build out an “industrial continuous waste plastic pyrolysis production line” capable of churning out 10,000 to 50,000 tons of plastic-derived SAF each year. That might seem like a lot to unpack, but it’s really pretty simple. Basically, the Chinese firm is announcing the development of a massive supply line that turns plastic garbage into usable jet fuel. It plans to do so using a thermal decomposition process called pyrolysis, in which plastic waste is heated to extraordinary temperatures in an oxygen-free environment. Though it isn’t new, pyrolysis remains something of a niche for the industrial-grade plastics-recycling space. For this approach to meet the growing need for SAF, it first has to graduate beyond the realm of small eco-startups into a widely-accepted industry standard — which appears to be Niutech’s goal. Unlike previous SAF initiatives involving plastics, this particular production line will all be under one roof, cutting down on the need for costly shipping and handling. The Niutech refinery is also said to make use of a smorgasbord of plastics like polyethylene, nylon, polypropylene, and ABS, all “without complex pre-treatment such as washing, sorting or drying” — streamlining the complex process of sorting plastic and converting it to refinable oil at this kind of scale. Should Niutech and its anonymous industry partner succeed in bringing the system online, it would undoubtedly serve as a constant source of plastics-based SAF for the global aviation industry, and as a role model for other refineries to follow. “Large-scale pyrolysis projects impose stringent requirements on continuous operational capability,” a Niutech spokesperson said in the company’s press release. “Niutech has focused on pyrolysis technology for nearly 40 years and brings mature equipment and extensive project experience. This partnership is another validation of the strength of our industrial continuous technology.” More on China: American Tech Companies Are Suddenly Sweating Bullets as China Catches Up on AI

Toronto Blue Jays Roasted for Posting AI Slop Promoting Nestle, Claiming It’s the Work of a Human Animator

Toronto Blue Jays Roasted for Posting AI Slop Promoting Nestle, Claiming It’s the Work of a Human Animator

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. The animation industry is facing an existential threat in the form of generative AI. Entertainment execs remain adamant that the tech is the answer, allowing fewer workers to accomplish more in less time — a thinly-veiled attempt, critics charge, to pinch pennies and churn out lower quality content. Marketing teams are also frequently caving to the irresistible draw of text-to-video generators, a cutting of corners in ways that are both uninspired and easy to spot. And as the backlash to generative AI continues to grow, especially in creative fields, those attempts are bringing in far more negative than positive attention. Case in point, fans of the Major League Baseball team the Toronto Blue Jays were outraged after the team’s social media account posted an animated short, as part of a brand tie-in with Nestle-owned beverage company Nestea. The clip, animated in a style typical for a TV show aimed at adults, like “Bojack Horseman” or “Family Guy,” bears all the hallmarks of the sloppy use of AI, from garbled, misoriented hands to illegible text. A community note appended to the post points out that the video “contains AI artifacts including garbled text on trading card boxes and a player with three arms in the rookie poster, contrary to the claim it was made by a real animator.” Instead of owning up to the lazy use of AI, the team dug in its heels. “Corny,” one fan wrote. “[Blue Jays owner] Rogers can’t afford real animation??” “Corny comment,” the team’s official account replied. “This was made by a real animator.” The doubling down in particular didn’t sit well with fans. “It’s not just AI, It’s really painfully obvious error riddled AI,” one user tweeted. “Yeahhh, you ain’t fooling anybody,” another wrote. “Do better.” “Plenty of talented animators you could have hired to do this instead of having it be AI slop,” one user commented on an extended version posted to YouTube. “I fear you got fleeced paying whoever made this, it’s very obvious [AI] was used especially with the background details,” another added. The incident perfectly illustrates widespread disillusionment with the use of generative AI and how radioactive the topic has become. We’ve gotten to the point where small businesses are starting to hand draw their own promotional signage and vow not to use any AI, as 404 Media reported last month — getting a major influx of interest as a result. It’s a particularly precarious situation for human animators, who’ve watched their industry change dramatically over the years. Even the creator of the iconic stop motion franchise “Wallace and Gromit” recently said he’d start to “embrace” AI. Leaning into the tech can easily backfire. Emmy-winning writer director Jorge Gutierrez announced he was partnering up with Amazon as part of its GenAI Creators’ Fund earlier this year, but the backlash turned out to be fierce enough for him to walk away from the deal altogether. More on AI animation: OpenAI’s Attempt at an AI-Generated Pixar-Style Movie Is in Shambles

Zohran Mamdani Bans AI for NYC Public School Students Up to Eighth Grade
2026-09-03

Zohran Mamdani Bans AI for NYC Public School Students Up to Eighth Grade

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. New York City just instituted a sweeping ban on schools using AI in the classroom through the start of high school, along with other restrictions on the technology. Mayor Zohran Mamdani announced the one-year moratorium on Wednesday, calling it the “most comprehensive of its kind anywhere in the country.” “The tech industry wants us to believe that AI in early education is not only inevitable, but that it is necessary,” Mamdani said during a press conference. “We do not see it that way. I have yet to see a study showing that AI is beneficial for students in elementary and middle school, with the exception, of course, of research sponsored by the very corporations that stand to profit.” Under the new AI policy, the school system will “fully discontinue or disable more” than 38 AI features that were part of previously allowed programs that don’t meet the new standards, Mamdani said. Ailish Brady, the deputy mayor’s senior education adviser, said that includes an AI reading assistant called Amira, Politico noted. Young students, starting with kids in 2-K (the city’s free education and daycare program for two-year-olds), won’t use AI as part of class until they enter high school. Even then, its use will be closely controlled. High schools will take limited “AI literacy” classes to prepare students for what Mamdani called an “AI-saturated world.” Exceptions will be made for helping students with learning disabilities or are still learning English. The restrictions also don’t apply to certain approved instructional programs like e-books and coding software. AI isn’t the only tech the policy grapples with. It also imposes strict limits on screen time, recommending no more than 30 minutes of using devices per day for students in third through fifth grade, and 45 minutes for middle schoolers. Beyond the broader debate raging on AI’s role in education — with colleges across the country embroiled in AI cheating scandals, as well as a bevy of research suggesting AI impairs critical thinking and other cognitive abilities —  the ban comes after months of New York City facing heated political debate on using the tech in the classroom. In April, controversy erupted over plans to open an AI-focused high school in Manhattan, with a crowd of parents, teachers, and high schoolers protesting outside City Hall. While the plans were withdrawn soon afterwards, it amplified calls on Mamdani to institute an AI moratorium. While deeming the moratorium a meaningful step in the right direction, some hoped Mamdani would’ve gone further. Kelly Clancy, the founder of Parents for AI Caution and a member of the Coalition for an AI Moratorium, wanted a temporary moratorium on AI in all grades. Some of the original calls for a moratorium demanded that it last at least two years. “The fact that this policy exists at all is due to the enormous work of parents across the city who spoke out against the overreach of AI into their kids’ classroom,” Clancy told the New York Times. The aggressive action will be closely watched by other states and school systems. New York City boasts the largest school system in the nation, with the ban affecting more than 600,000 students. It will be a large, and indeed, “comprehensive” experiment in how detoxing young minds on AI will affect their learning — though it pales in size to the experiment that tech companies are conducting by unleashing the tech on students in the first place. “Children need their teachers and human connection in order to learn and in order to grow,” Mamdani said. “And they need to develop skills alongside their peers, build relationships with their teachers, and wrestle with tough problems on their own.” More on education: Scientists Gave 12-Year-Olds Access to an AI Tutor and You Probably Already Know What They Did With It

TechCrunch
The sameness problem behind those unappetizing AI-generated menus
2026-09-04

The sameness problem behind those unappetizing AI-generated menus

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
When it first happens to you, you think you’re crazy. You wander into a cafe and look at a menu with a variety of bagel sandwiches, but each illustration looks eerily flawless, precisely symmetrical, and oddly smooth, eliciting a visceral sensation that something isn’t right. You might think you’re paranoid, but you’re not losing your mind. Generative AI menus have hit the restaurant business courtesy of models trained on a narrow, “pleasing” aesthetic that feels wrong even when you can’t articulate why. Sometimes, these illustrations are egregiously fake, like a burrito with cheese so bubbly and melty that it looks more like avant garde art than lunch. More often, they’re so ordinary looking that you only notice something is wrong when you take a second to look more closely. “It’s almost like an alien trying to make a pizza without understanding its core principles,” Reality Defender CTO Alex Lisle told TechCrunch. (Reality Defender itself is part of a growing category of startups selling AI-detection and content-verification tools — a business that exists in part because of issues like this one.) Throw a rock in NYC and you’ll hit a mildly off putting AI food advertisement. pic.twitter.com/lLXVkFIFoG Lisle says that the way these models are built can help explain why illustrations seem to embrace such a specific aesthetic — one where every ice cream scoop is perfectly round, and where shrimp seem to have been genetically modified to eat their own tails, creating new “Lovecraftian food horrors.” Large language models (LLMs) and diffusion models — the kinds of AI models that make seemingly omniscient chatbots and image generators like ChatGPT and Midjourney possible — are trained on vast quantities of data. The models then identify patterns in the datasets to predict what a user is looking for when they ask something like, “Make me a menu for a burger restaurant.” “A lot of this stuff looks like a Chili’s menu from 2015, and there’s a reason for that,” Lisle said. “That was the corpus of work from which [the models] drew their function.” New training data is invaluable to the companies building AI models — Amazon has even been found to source rare books to scan and add to its training data, only to destroy those books once they’ve been uploaded. It’s inevitable that some AI-generated content will seep into these incomprehensibly large data sets. But when AI models train on too much of their own AI-generated content, they risk model collapse. “Model collapse is almost like a mad cow disease… when you feed the outputs from one model back into itself, eventually the inbreeding becomes too much, and the whole thing collapses,” Lisle explained. “What we see here is convergence, which isn’t necessarily model collapse.” Convergence is a bit less extreme, degrading the quality of an AI’s outputs without making it entirely useless. If someone asks an AI model to generate a menu for a fast food restaurant, the model will likely reference menus from Wendy’s, Burger King, McDonald’s, or another popular chain. These menus already share a similar style, which means that the AI-generated outputs will mimic that same style, only to further reinforce it further if the AI-generated menu ends up back in training data. But menus and advertisements for food will always look better than the real thing, like a Big Mac in a McDonald’s commercial where each layer of the sandwich is arranged by a prop designer to look maximally appetizing. This effect can become even more pronounced in AI outputs. “The optimization of the data sets is for pleasingness, or you know, not being offensive, and so there’s a way that turns into homogenization,” Lee Rainie, Director of the Imagining the Digital Future Center at Elon University, told TechCrunch. “What AI is known to do both in images and language is to shave off the edges.” On a more localized scale, this smoothing of images seems to happen when you use an AI image generator to create a menu and apply edits to it. On X, a user named Labtec showed what happens when you make a menu in ChatGPT, then edit it 100 times to see how the food continues to look less and less like it should. (We replicated the experiment and found similar results.) “The end result actually makes me uncomfortable,” Labtec wrote. I made a restaurant menu in ChatGPT then edited it 100 times to see how those hideous slop menus end up the way they are. The end result actually makes me uncomfortable pic.twitter.com/sDIUKlabdp Restaurants are likely falling victim to this problem, revising their AI-generated menus to alter small details over and over, like prices or item names. It seems that with each edit, the food images become a tiny bit more round and smooth. “People have an almost unexplainable sense about when they’re looking at something that’s AI-generated, compared with something that was real in the first place,” Rainie said. “There’s just a sensibility that people sometimes find hard to articulate, but they kind of know it when they see it and I think that’s one of the reasons why some of the early stories about the backlash [against restaurants using AI menus] is so pronounced.” There’s science behind our aversion to these AI menus. Researchers at the University of Duisburg-Essen in Germany found that AI-generated food images exhibited an “uncanny valley” effect, where images of food that looked almost real elicited more disgust and unease than images that were obviously fake. That squeamishness only intensifies in light of the cultural context around AI. If people react to these images so negatively, then that’s probably reason enough for restaurants to stop trying to make AI menus work. But the issues that bring us perfectly browned hamburger buns extend beyond the dinner table. “Seeing and hearing has always been believing, to the point where even our court systems are entirely tuned to the idea that the gold standard in evidence is taped confessions and videotaped evidence,” Lisle said. “That’s no longer the case. The world has fundamentally shifted, for good or for ill.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Oura files to go public
2026-09-03

Oura files to go public

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Oura, the smart ring maker, has filed to go public. The filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Thursday shows that Oura’s revenue has jumped substantially in the past year. The company went from $697 million in revenue during the nine-month period that ended June 30 last year to $1.2 billion during the corresponding period this year. The company has said previously that it generated $500 million in revenue in 2024, roughly $1 billion in 2025, and that it expected to generate close to $2 billion in revenue this year. The company says it has sold 3.6 million rings over the past year, and that it currently has approximately 5 million paid members — that is, users who subscribe to its service for broader health metrics. The filing also says that Oura has an approximately 85% weighted-average 12-month membership retention rate, meaning that roughly 85% of members who sign up in a given month are still subscribed a year later. The company’s rings, which sell in the range of $350 to $400, are, at their most fundamental level, fitness trackers designed to measure a user’s biometrics — everything from metabolism and heart rate to stress levels and sleep patterns. Paired with an app, the ring and that software are marketed by Oura as an “always-on health intelligence platform.” Late last month, it was reported that Oura was looking to raise $3 billion during its expected public offering. The company, which was founded in Finland in 2013, confidentially filed for an IPO in May. Bloomberg previously reported that the company is expected to seek a $16 billion valuation. In October of last year, it had been valued at around $11 billion. In its SEC filing, Oura outlines where it believes its audience can widen in the coming years. “We believe our opportunity extends beyond traditional wearable use cases centered on activity and fitness tracking,” it says. “We believe our platform can support significantly larger populations as we continue to expand access, build clinical evidence, and deepen integrations with health plans, employers, and care providers.” Oura also notes how its trove of data is fueling new AI integrations. “We believe we have amassed one of the largest and highest-quality longitudinal biometric datasets in consumer health, tracking over 50 health and wellness metrics and representing nearly 42 billion hours of physiological data,” it states. “This dataset powers our AI and machine-learning models, which decode complex physiological patterns and improve in accuracy, personalization, and predictive capability as member histories deepen.” TechCrunch has reached out to Oura for more information. The company, which now has offices throughout the world, including San Francisco, was recently hit with a proposed class action lawsuit accusing it of misleading users about the accuracy of its sleep tracking capabilities. The suit alleges Oura’s rings can’t actually detect the physiological signals needed to determine sleep stages, and instead rely on AI-generated estimates that the complaint describes as little more reliable than a coin flip. The litigation follows years of online complaints from users who said that Oura consistently rated their sleep as optimal when, in fact, it was not. The company has disputed the allegations and previously told TechCrunch it will “defend against” the claims in the “appropriate legal forum.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Tesla is asking people if they want to buy and run Cybercab fleets
2026-09-03

Tesla is asking people if they want to buy and run Cybercab fleets

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Tesla published a form on Thursday for businesses interested in buying Cybercab fleets or providing infrastructure for its network, the latest sign that the company’s aspirations for its gold-hued autonomous vehicle stretch beyond being a robotaxi operator. The robotaxi interest form, which was released ahead of the company’s Cybercab event in Austin, is not definitive proof that Tesla will sell its autonomous vehicles to third-party operators. But it’s certainly an indicator of where the company’s longer-term plans lie. Tesla wants to scale and it doesn’t seem to want to do it alone. Tesla CEO Elon Musk has talked often, and for years, about building a massive fleet of low-cost robotaxis. But in the early days, those dreams centered on personally owned Tesla vehicles. As early as 2016, Musk spoke publicly about a future in which Tesla owners, equipped with self-driving software, would be able to earn money by renting out their vehicles. He stuck with that Tesla network idea for years, noting at the company’s Autonomy Day in 2019 that it would allow owners to add their autonomous vehicles to its ride-sharing app, similar to how Uber’s business model works. “I feel very confident predicting that there will be autonomous robotaxis from Tesla next year — not in all jurisdictions because we won’t have regulatory approval everywhere,” Musk said in 2020. That vision never materialized. Instead, the company has focused on testing, and now operating, its own fleet of robotaxis — first with Tesla Model Y vehicles and now the purpose-built Cybercab. Until now, Tesla seemed committed to keeping its robotaxi business in-house. The interest form, which says “helps us build our robotaxi network,” suggests the company sees promise and profits in widening the circle to include third-party companies. What that might look like, though, isn’t defined. The company asks interested parties to pick one of several possible options, including Cybercab fleet purchasing, mobility hubs and infrastructure, event collaboration, and “other.” There are a growing number of companies jumping into the robotaxi fleet management business. For instance, Moove, an African fintech startup that initially focused vehicle financing for ride-hailing drivers, is scaling up an autonomous fleet management business. The startup, which raised $250 million last month at a $2.1 billion valuation, is the fleet operator for Waymo in Phoenix, Miami, and Las Vegas, and in the future, London. The company doesn’t own the Waymo vehicles, but its CEO told TechCrunch that it plans to. Other autonomous fleet management companies, which Uber has partnered with in its bid to own a piece of the robotaxi market, include Avomo and New Horizon as well as larger more traditional rental car giants like Avis and Hertz. Tesla’s welcome mat to fleet operators could encourage more small players to open up shop — helping the company saturate markets faster. TechCrunch
Startup ARR is less secure than ever, new research shows
2026-09-03

Startup ARR is less secure than ever, new research shows

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
AI has ushered in a lot of never-happened-before moments, but one of the most transformative is its impact on enterprise IT. Companies that have historically been cautious and committed long-term to what they buy are on pace to spend $4.25 trillion on technology in 2026, market researcher IDC predicts. It’s almost all driven by AI. New research from venture capital firm Madrona shows that 74% of 150 enterprise IT professionals it surveyed plan to expand their AI budgets in the next 12 months, and the rest plan to hold spending steady. Yet these same enterprises say that fewer than half of their AI pilots ever make it into full production. That’s actually an improvement. Last year, MIT famously reported that 95% of enterprise AI projects had failed in terms of ROI. Fewer than half succeeding is a pretty low bar, but it’s better than a 5% success rate. But the most telling finding from Madrona’s report is that, even when an enterprise does roll out the AI tech, it doesn’t commit to it long term. Some 77% of enterprises reevaluate their AI vendors every six months or even on a rolling basis. “This creates a ‘fast in, fast out’ dynamic that is fundamentally different from traditional enterprise SaaS, where multi-year contracts provided a moat of inertia,” Madrona writes in the report. “In enterprise AI, switching costs are lower and the re-evaluation cadence is relentless.” This has widespread implications for all those fast-growing annual recurring revenue (ARR) numbers startups report. Enterprise trial budgets are what fueled the initial AI boom of 2025. This year was supposed to be the year these big customers settled in and started committing long term to AI startups. Enterprise contracts are what allow so many AI startups to claim astronomically fast revenue growth — think the phenomenon of startups going from $0-$10 million in three months. Yet, for the first time ever, enterprise revenue remains insecure, even after a startup’s AI product graduates out of a pilot phase and gets adopted by a company. Part of the issue is that many AI startups haven’t fully landed on a good way to price their AI wares for enterprises. New research from VC firm Andreessen Horowitz that surveyed 50 technical AI buyers found that more than half of them want AI fees tied to the work produced or other outcomes, rather than to usage like the number of tokens consumed. Charging for usage like tokens is basically a SaaS-era business model. Once an enterprise knows it needs email, or HR software, or cloud storage, it’s merely a matter of how many employees or how much data it must pay for. For AI, pricing “around the recognizable work” is what helps the startup prove its worth to the customer. When the fees revolve around, say, how many reports are processed, or tickets closed, or leads generated, this makes the product “economically valuable to both sides,” writes a16z partners Tugce Erten and Sarah Wang. All of this means that AI has potentially ushered in a new era of enterprise experimentation. That opens doors to startups — enterprises are more willing to try their tech — but it also means an enterprise contract no longer secures long-term revenue. When or if enterprises will revert to their long-term buying habits remains to be seen. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TechCrunch
The Cybercab is Tesla’s ‘fork in the road’ moment
2026-09-03

The Cybercab is Tesla’s ‘fork in the road’ moment

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Today could be the day Tesla changes forever. In a couple of hours, Tesla will “launch” the Cybercab — a small, gold, two-seater sedan with no steering wheel or pedals that uses cameras and AI to navigate the world — in its hometown of Austin, Texas. The company hasn’t said what the launch will entail, exactly. It could be a few cars offering rides in a small area downtown, or a far grander affair in both size and scope. Tesla certainly wants you to believe it’s the latter. In the runup to the event, Tesla staged dozens — perhaps more — of its Cybercabs around Austin and in many other U.S. cities. At the time this story was published, 420 autonomous vehicles were registered in Texas, according to the state’s automated vehicle tracker. Ashok Elluswamy, the head of Tesla AI, recently said in a post on X that the “streets won’t be the same anymore.” When another user wrote that Tesla is “about to flood Austin,” CEO Elon Musk replied: “Yes.” On Wednesday night, Musk posted a seemingly AI-generated photo of the Cybercab consuming the city alongside a massive dust storm. “A Storm of Cybercabs,” Musk wrote in another post. These gravitas-laced posts mark a departure from the company’s go-slow-and-safe messaging in the months leading up to the event. Musk and other Tesla executives had previously professed they were taking a cautious approach to its robotaxis in the name of safety, though at times they seemed more concerned about public perception of a potential failure. Of course, Tesla’s launch strategy isn’t as meaningful as the outcome. Tesla’s ability to successfully build a robotaxi network around the Cybercab would provide the first tangible evidence that it has transitioned from an automaker with some side projects to an AI and robotics company. There’s a trope Musk favors that is apt for this moment: the fork in the road. It was the subject of an email he sent to Twitter employees in 2022 when he gave them an ultimatum to stay or leave. He invoked it again in early 2025 when he encouraged federal government employees to leave as part of his job leading DOGE, before he truly started wrecking house. Musk loves the metaphor so much that he commissioned a 30-foot-tall sculpture of a literal fork on a literal road for Burning Man in 2022, before moving the sculpture to a Tesla office. The Cybercab is Tesla’s fork in the road. This is either the moment the company begins to transform urban transportation as we know it, or the moment that reveals that, despite its many efforts, Tesla really is just an automaker after all. Musk first revealed the Cybercab in 2024 at a Hollywood movie lot. But the idea has been around far longer. Musk has spent more than a decade promising that Tesla would find a way to make its cars fully autonomous, paving the way for a network of robotaxis. He even went so far as to claim, in 2016, that every Tesla being made had the capability to become fully autonomous and that the cars only needed the right software update to make it happen. He was wrong. Tesla has reworked the hardware in its cars multiple times since that declaration, and Musk recently admitted that millions of them will need some kind of hardware retrofit to become autonomous. Still, Tesla kept developing driving automation software. It has steadily released and refined its primary offering, Full Self-Driving (Supervised). But as capable as that software has become, it is still driver-assistance software. The responsibility and liability remain with the driver. The Cybercab is supposed to change all that. It emerged from an effort by Tesla to develop a next-generation EV platform that was cheaper to build. The plan was for the company to take what it learned from the Model 3 and Model Y — its two biggest mass-market successes — and develop a new platform that would cost half as much, allowing Tesla to make more affordable EVs. Years of reporting and Musk’s own biography have shown that he was so dead set on achieving full autonomy that he decided against building a manually drivable car on the platform. Instead, Musk focused entirely on using the new platform to power the two-seater robotaxi we now know as the Cybercab. In April 2024, months ahead of the Cybercab reveal, he approved massive layoffs at Tesla in service of going “balls to the wall for autonomy,” which he called a “blindingly obvious move.” “Everything else is like variations on a horse carriage,” he wrote at the time. Tesla started testing the first version of its Robotaxi program last June in Austin using modified Model Y SUVs. For the first few months, the company had an employee in the front seat (sometimes behind the wheel, sometimes in the passenger seat) who could stop the car if something went wrong. Things have gone wrong along the way, though not catastrophically. Tesla has reported a few dozen incidents in Austin since it launched the Robotaxi pilot. Most were minor crashes with stationary objects, and a few involved teleoperators remotely moving the vehicles at low speeds. The company has expanded this Model Y-based system to a few other cities in Texas and Florida. But over the past year, the network has not lived up to the hype Tesla manufactured at launch in June 2025. In July, the company revealed that the number of paid miles the network was facilitating was actually moving backward. But that may not matter much to Tesla, which believes scaling its robotaxi network requires the Cybercab. Tesla designed the Cybercab around cost. It’s smaller, lighter, and has a lower-capacity battery pack than any other Tesla. Fewer raw materials — especially on the battery side — make it cheaper to build. The cheaper it is to build, the faster Tesla can potentially turn a profit once it’s deployed. That math has Tesla’s most ardent fans giddy. They look at Tesla’s ability to build dozens of Cybercabs for roughly the same amount of money Waymo spends to purchase and outfit fewer than 10 of its newest “Ojai” vans and see it as proof that the robotaxi war is already won. Musk seems to agree. In a reply to a post that included a graphical representation of this argument, he wrote, “this doesn’t even take into account the operational efficiency of Cybercab.” There are caveats worth considering. Tesla has, to date, only operated its robotaxi network within a geofence in each city. While some are larger than others, and the company has kept expanding some of them, Musk himself once said: “If you need a geofenced area, you don’t have real self-driving.” Then there’s the challenge of operating the network at scale. Tesla has so far benefited from having a small network in just a few cities. That naturally means its self-driving software encounters fewer edge cases than Waymo, which has 4,000 robotaxis on the road. Fewer trips and fewer edge cases also mean fewer chances for riders to spot problems and post them on social media or report them to regulators or journalists. Tesla has also benefited from the fact that its fleet has consisted of Model Y SUVs, which are harder to distinguish on the road than a bright white Jaguar I-Pace kitted out with sensors. That will change with the Cybercabs. If Tesla’s software isn’t up to the task, its failures will be much harder to miss when they happen in shiny gold cars. How Americans and Canadians Are Feeling About the Escalating Trade War
2026-09-03

How Americans and Canadians Are Feeling About the Escalating Trade War

TIME | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings American President Donald Trump greets Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney during a summit on Gaza in Sharm el-Sheikh on Oct. 13, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With relations between the U.S. and Canada becoming more and more strained following the breakdown in trade talks between the two countries, most Canadians are backing their government and the way it has chosen to handle the standoff. But on the other side of the border, Americans are offering the Trump Administration far less support for its approach. Trade negotiations between the U.S. and Canada collapsed last month, with each side placing the blame on the other. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney criticized the U.S. for requesting what he described as “last-minute changes” that he argued were “unfair, uneconomic, and called into question the reliability of any deal.” American President Donald Trump, meanwhile, accused Canada of “‘Ripping Off’ the U.S.A. for decades” and portrayed the country as being “difficult and unreasonable.” The day after trade talks broke down, the Trump Administration’s 50% tariffs on billions of dollars worth of Canadian products took effect. Canada then announced retaliatory tariffs that will go into effect on Sept. 8. Tensions between the two nations have only continued to grow. Last week, Trump signed an Executive Order directing his Interior Secretary to change the name of Lake Ontario to “Lake America”—a move that angered Canadians and sparked pushback from political leaders in both Canada and the U.S. There is no clear resolution in sight for the rift in the countries’ critical trading relationship, with both Trump and Carney remaining firm in their stances. While speaking to reporters on Tuesday, Carney signaled that he would be willing to resume negotiations—but only if U.S. officials stop insulting and mocking his country. “When the Americans stop doing memes, stop throwing shade, stop trying to be tough, and start being serious about having those discussions, we can have those discussions,” the Canadian leader said. “It’s not constructive,” he added. “But that’s their democracy.” Trump, meanwhile, doubled down on his accusations against Canada when asked the following day about the prospect of talks starting back up. “Canada has been ripping off the United States for many, many years, for decades, many decades,” the President said during a Wednesday press conference. Here’s what recent polls reveal about how Americans and Canadians feel about the heated situation—and how their leaders are handling it. Most Americans—nearly 70%—said they feel that the U.S. should be open to reaching a compromise with Canada, even if that leads to many of the U.S.’s demands not being met, according to an Ipsos poll that was released on Tuesday. In comparison, only 25% of Americans said they believe that the U.S. should take a firm stand against its northern neighbor and push for many of its demands to be met. Americans were also more likely to hold their own government responsible for the breakdown in relations than Canada’s. About 46% said they think that the U.S. shoulders more of the blame for the ongoing trade war, while only 12% said they believe that Canada is more culpable and 18% said they feel that both parties are equally at fault, according to the poll. The survey also found that most Americans oppose actions that could or have already escalated tensions between the two countries. For instance, nearly 60% of Americans said they are against the U.S. imposing additional tariffs on Canada, while only 20% indicated support for such an aggressive step. And more than 60% of Americans said they disagree with Trump’s order to rename Lake Ontario, while only 14% backed the move. Americans’ objection to the Executive Order in particular was seen across party lines—84% of Democrats and 65% of independents said they were against it, and more Republicans (43%) objected to it than those who agreed with it (33%). A recent Economist/YouGov poll produced similar results: nearly 60% of Americans who responded to the survey said they were against increasing tariffs on Canadian products, compared to less than 30% who said they would back such a move. About 45% said they think Canada has not been unfair to the U.S. when it comes to trading between the two nations, while just 28% are aligned with Trump in thinking that Canada has been unfair. The majority—64%—of Americans still describe Canada as an ally or friendly, according to the Economist/YouGov poll. But while they remain a minority, the portion of Americans who view Canada in a negative light has grown in recent years; about 22% describe Canada as either unfriendly or an enemy, a record high since 2016. That growth has mostly been driven by Americans who identify as Republicans, the survey found. Amid the standoff with Canada, Trump’s approval rating has remained at around 38%, where it has hovered in recent months. That figure marks a stark 14-point drop from where his approval rating stood when he retook the White House last January. The majority of Canadians—63%—said they feel that the country made the right choice to maintain its stance and walk away from trade talks with the U.S., “even if it meant higher costs and job losses,” according to an Ipsos poll conducted for Global News that was released on Aug. 29. Even more Canadians—73%—said they agreed with the nation’s decision to launch retaliatory tariffs on American products worth billions of dollars. The poll did find, though, that support for those actions was lower among younger demographics than it was among older age groups. For instance, a little less than 50% of Gen Z adults backed Canada’s decision to remain firm in trade negotiations, compared to 80% for elderly boomers. Another poll, conducted by the Canadian nonprofit Angus Reid Institute, found that about 76% of Canadians believe the country was right to suspend trade talks, and that 69% said they believe that Carney demonstrated “strength” by turning down a “bad deal.” A majority of Canadians—62%—also indicated support for retaliatory tariffs against the U.S., calling the move “about right under the circumstances.” Still, 89% of Canadians expressed concerns that the trade war with the U.S. would affect prices, the poll found—though more than 60% of Canadians said they think the country will eventually come out of the dispute stronger. As Trump’s approval rating has remained underwater, the Carney government’s increased by four points to 56% in recent weeks, while its disapproval rating has dropped two points to just 29%, according to data shared by the polling firm Abacus Data on Sunday. Approval for Carney individually has also improved recently, with 53% of Canadians having a positive opinion of him and only 28% having a negative view of him. The polling firm said that its findings indicate that “the Carney government’s political standing is built primarily on how Canadians judge its management of the American relationship.” The Canadian Liberal Party, which Carney is the leader of, has preserved its narrow majority in Parliament, with its candidates emerging victorious in three special elections earlier this week. Carney cited his government’s actions in the trade war as a driving reason for the victories. “What’s a clear message from last night in three very different ridings across this great diverse country is that Canadians are behind the plan of the government, which is first and foremost about building strong at home, diversifying with reliable partners, building Canada strong for all,” he said on Tuesday.

‘I Wouldn’t Call it a War’: Vance Downplays Conflict With Iran, Even as Strikes Continue
2026-09-03

‘I Wouldn’t Call it a War’: Vance Downplays Conflict With Iran, Even as Strikes Continue

TIME | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Vice President JD Vance speaks at the first press briefing the White House has held in over 40 days, on September 03, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Vice President J.D. Vance said Thursday that he would not characterize the United States as being at war with Iran, even as the two countries have exchanged strikes in recent days and the Trump Administration has declined to say when a conflict now stretching beyond six months might finally end. “I wouldn’t call it a war,” Vance told reporters at a White House press briefing, the first since Karoline Leavitt stepped down as press secretary last month. “Right now, there is no active shooting.” The distinction was striking after a renewed burst of hostilities this week, including American strikes inside Iran and Iranian attacks in the region. But it echoed an argument that President Trump and other Republicans made in the earliest days of the conflict: that the United States was carrying out a limited military operation rather than embarking on another prolonged Middle Eastern war, which, under the Constitution, would require congressional approval. More than six months later, that distinction has become increasingly difficult to sustain. The United States has repeatedly bombed targets inside Iran as Tehran attacks commercial shipping vessels through the Strait of Hormuz, contributing to higher energy prices. Vance acknowledged what he called a recent “flare-up” but argued that the most intensive phase of the American campaign had ended long ago. “Major combat operations are currently not ongoing, and they haven’t been ongoing in a very long time,” he said. He said Operation Epic Fury, the large-scale campaign that followed last year’s Operation Midnight Hammer, lasted roughly six weeks. The earlier operation, he said, destroyed Iranian nuclear facilities, while Epic Fury severely damaged Iran’s conventional military and its ability to produce weapons and rebuild its nuclear program. But Vance offered little indication of when the broader confrontation might end, saying the Administration would not commit itself to a timeline while Iran continued threatening commercial shipping and global energy supplies. Asked whether the conflict would be over before November’s midterm elections, Vance said he did not know. “You would have to ask the Iranians,” he said, adding: “When will the Iranians stop shooting at ships?” The answer underscored the open-ended nature of a conflict that Trump and others in the Administration repeatedly predicted would be brief. As recently as Wednesday, the President said he did not believe it would last “much longer.” But his Administration is simultaneously intensifying economic pressure on Iran, maintaining a naval blockade, and reserving the option of further military strikes if Iran attacks American interests or interferes with commercial shipping. Presidents have long resisted the “war” label in conflicts undertaken without a formal declaration of war. Harry Truman called the American intervention in Korea a United Nations “police action,” while Bill Clinton avoided describing NATO’s bombing campaign in Kosovo as a war. Barack Obama similarly argued that the 2011 intervention in Libya did not constitute a “war” requiring congressional authorization. In March, Trump openly acknowledged that referring to the conflict as “war” carried legal significance because Congress had not authorized it. “I won’t use the word ‘war’ because they say, if you use the word war, that’s maybe not a good thing to do,” he told House Republicans at the time. “They don’t like the word ‘war,’ because you’re supposed to get approval, so I’ll use the word ‘military operation,’ which is really what it is.” House Speaker Mike Johnson similarly insisted shortly after the initial strikes that the country was “not at war,” describing the campaign instead as “a very specific, clear mission.” Democrats have rejected that characterization and repeatedly tried to force votes requiring congressional authorization for continued hostilities. Those efforts have failed largely along party lines. The dispute has revived a decades-old argument over presidential war powers and the extent to which a president can sustain military operations abroad without explicit approval from Congress. Vance said Thursday that the administration would not rely on Iran to negotiate in good faith and would instead use whatever tools it considered necessary to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon and to protect global energy markets. He argued that Iran’s grip on the Strait of Hormuz was “effectively gone” and that oil traffic through the crucial waterway was nearing its pre-conflict level. Shipping data, however, shows traffic remains well below where it stood before the fighting began. The Administration has made restoring commercial traffic through the strait a central objective as energy prices have become an increasing political liability ahead of the midterms. Vance acknowledged the pain of higher gasoline prices but argued that they could have risen substantially more without American intervention in Iran. “Gas, frankly, could have been much, much higher were it not for our efforts,” he said. He declined to predict when prices might return to around $3 a gallon, saying the more important objective was ensuring “that Iran doesn’t have a nuclear weapon.” The Administration is now pursuing what amounts to a strategy of intermittent military force combined with sustained economic pressure. Last week, it announced “Operation Economic Outcast,” an effort to further isolate Iran from its remaining trading partners. But the campaign has so far produced limited results, and imposing sanctions aggressive enough to cripple Iranian trade could require confronting countries including China, India, and Russia. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said this week that the Administration expected the principal pressure on Iran going forward to be economic, while reserving the right to use military force when necessary. Trump himself has resumed publicly encouraged Iranians to challenge the regime, writing on Truth Social on Tuesday, “When are the Iranian people going to rise up and fight?” When asked by TIME on Thursday whether the Trump Administration was considering arming Iranian dissidents or providing them with other direct assistance, Vance declined to provide more details. “Everything that could happen is on the table: economic pressure, military pressure, diplomatic pressure, covert pressure,” he said. “Of course, these are tools in the President's toolkit.” Iranian leaders, meanwhile, have shown little sign of capitulating. Its leadership remains divided between officials advocating negotiations and hard-liners pressing for confrontation, and Tehran has continued striking American interests and allies in response to U.S. attacks. The renewed American bombing has also brought fresh scrutiny of civilian casualties. Iranian authorities said a U.S. strike this week hit a wedding celebration in Sirik in southern Iran, killing at least five people and wounding scores more, including women and children. A New York Times visual analysis and a weapons expert concluded that the wedding was struck by a bomb released by American forces during attacks in the area. Vance said the episode was under investigation but expressed deep skepticism about Iran’s account. “The Iranian state media has not been a very good scribe about what’s happened in the conflict thus far,” he said. “So I’m extremely skeptical of this.” “Obviously, sometimes things happen,” he added. “When our military makes mistakes, they learn from them to try to get better from them, so we’re investigating it very fully.”

The Stakes of America’s 2026 Midterm Elections
2026-09-03

The Stakes of America’s 2026 Midterm Elections

TIME | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A sign outside a polling location during a primary election in Marblehead, Massachusetts, US, on Tuesday, Sept. 1, 2026. Five years ago, in the wake of the Jan. 6 attack on the Capitol, America’s civic institutions pledged to do more to defend our system of fair and free elections. Major philanthropies increased their investments in programs that defend democracy. Law firms expanded their pro bono election-protection work. University presidents publicly condemned the effort to thwart the will of American voters. Many of the country’s largest businesses pledged to stop donating to candidates who had tried to overturn an election. Today, our elections are again under attack. But while some institutions have maintained their commitment to defending democracy, far too many have retreated. There are fewer than 100 days until the 2026 nationwide elections. Now is the time for everyone to protect the system of fair and free elections upon which our society and prosperity ultimately depend. Together, the two of us have spent considerable time in all the major sectors of American society: philanthropy, business, academia, and the law. We understand that leaders in these sectors fear government retribution, and understandably so. But with the integrity of our elections under enormous strain—and with government officials signaling that more aggressive attacks may follow—it is time for all institutions to reaffirm their role as defenders of the free and fair elections at the heart of American democracy. Defending our democracy begins with a clear-eyed assessment of the threats to it. Over the past 18 months, the Justice Department has disbanded government agencies meant to protect elections from foreign interference and to help states administer them smoothly. President Donald Trump has repeatedly made false claims that the 2020 election was fraudulent and made new ones—without evidence—about the midterms. Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin recently threatened to jail local election officials his department finds were insufficiently responsive to the federal government’s unproven claims of fraud.  Overall, the federal government has tried to wrest control of elections from the states, with courts repeatedly finding such efforts illegal. Now, experts across the country are deeply concerned about what might occur in the coming months, whether it’s false allegations that cast doubt on individual races, state officials refusing to certify election results as required by law, or baseless efforts to block House members from taking their seats in January. The good news is the success of these efforts to undermine the midterms is not inevitable. To the contrary, enormous work has already been done to protect the integrity of November’s election from those who would undermine it. Now, it is time for leaders who haven’t yet joined that effort to get off the sidelines no matter what their politics are. Losing our system of fair and free elections would not just affect one element of American life. It would affect all elements of American life. There are many ways for institutions to meet the moment and keep our country’s elections free and fair. First, many leaders, particularly those from the business community, meet regularly with members of the executive branch and Congress. They should use these relationships to remind elected officials of the need to respect election results no matter who wins or loses. These nonpartisan principles are the basic preconditions for a functioning democracy and should be respected by all. Second, many institutional leaders have strong ties to state and local officials. Now is the time to remind state legislators that attempting to disenfranchise eligible voters via last-minute changes to election rules, baseless claims of fraud, or partisan attempts to deny certification harm our democracy and create the kind of turmoil that negatively affects a state’s reputation and business climate. Third, leaders should forcefully support the local election administrators who run our elections. These are not professional politicians. They are our neighbors, co-workers, and friends—and recently, they have far too often been subject to threats and harassment by those who wish to undermine their nonpartisan mission. Institutional leaders can help elections run smoothly by encouraging their employees to become poll workers, and by supporting nonpartisan civic organizations that defend election officials in performing their duties, or that bring Americans from across the political spectrum together to support the right of all Americans to have their vote counted. Standing up for fair and free elections is not without risk. But with the foundation of our democracy under unprecedented assault and our basic rights on the line, the far greater risk is ignoring the threat to our country until it is too late. One day, leaders of America’s institutions will face the same profound question as every other citizen who loves this country. Future generations will ask, “Where were you when the stakes were highest?” TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.
